\section{Functions}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Function}  we call a relation ``\textbf{f}'' a ``\textbf{function}'' when every input has exactly one
    output i.e.
    \[ Func(f) : Rel(f) \land \forall x ,y_1 , y_2 \Big((x,y_1) \in f \wedge (x,y_2) \in f \rightarrow y_1 = y_2 \Big) \]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
    \textbf{$(schema)$} $(Func I, Func E )$

    \begin{tabular}{|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|}
        \hline
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \open
            \have  [ ]{2} {   x f y }
            \have  [ ]{2} {   x f z }
            \have [ ] {2} { \vdots }
            \have [ ] {2} { y = z }
            \close
            \have [ ] {2} { \therefore  Func(f) }      \by{$Func I$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}

         &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have [ ] {2} {  Func(f) }
            \hypo   [ ]{2} {   t f s }
            \hypo   [ ]{2} {   t f r }
            \have [ ] {2} {\therefore s = r }    \by{$Func E$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \\
        \hline
    \end{tabular}
    \\
    \\
    \\
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
\end{theorem}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Restriction of a function}   to a set ``$X$'' (usually a subset of ``$dom(f)$'') is
    the function
    \[ f\upharpoonright X := \{(x, y) \in f \mid x \in X \} \]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Output of a function} assume a function \textbf{f} and  \textbf{$(x,y) \in f$},
    we define ``$f(x):= y$'' and we call ``$f(x)$'' a ``\textbf{output of a function}''.
    and the reverse is also true, if we use ``$f(x)$'',
    we have a function  ``\textbf{f}'' and we can say  ``$x \in Dom(f)$''.
    ``output of a function'' is object too.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
    \textbf{$(schema)$} $(\forall I ,\forall E ,\exists I , \exists E)$

    \begin{tabular}{|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|}
        \hline

        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have [ ] {2} {  Func(f) }
            \hypo   [ ]{2} {   (x,y) \in f }
            \have [ ] {2} {\therefore f(x)=y}    \by{$f() E$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}

         &

        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have  [ ]  {2} {f(x)=y}
            \hypo [ ] {2} {  Func(f) }

            \have [ ] {2} {\therefore (x,y) \in f }    \by{$f() E$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \\
        \hline
    \end{tabular}

\end{theorem}

\begin{Attention}
    \textbf{} input of a function can be a ordered pair itself, so we can have $f((x,y))$ and it is a ``\textbf{output of a function}''.
    we define ``$f(x,y):=f((x,y))$'' for simplicity.
    this is true for tuples as well, so we can have ``$f((x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n}))$'' and we define
    ``$f(x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n}):=f((x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n}))$'' for simplicity.
\end{Attention}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Term} we define term like this.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item every obj is a term.
        \item  if $t_{0},...t_{n}$ are terms and ``$Func(f)$'' and ``$(t_{0},...t_{n}) \in
                  Dom(f)$''. then ``$f(t_{0},...t_{n})$'' is also a term.
    \end{enumerate}
    we denote terms with small letters like $t,s,r,...$.
\end{definition}

%
\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Substitution for term} we define Substitution an term with variable  like this.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item for variables if $x=y$ then $y[t/x]:=t$ and if $x \not= y$ then $y[t/x]:=y$.
        \item for constants $c[t/x]:=c$.
        \item for terms like $f(t_{0},...t_{n})$
              \[ f(t_{0},...t_{n})[t/x] := f(t_{0}[t/x],...t_{n}[t/x])\]
    \end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Substitution for formulas} we define Substitution for a formula like this.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item for atomic formulas we define it like this. if it's a single term we do it as
              we explain in ``\textbf{Substitution for term}''. if it's like $t_0=t_1$ or
              $t_0 \in t_1$ ($t_0 , t_1$ are terms). we define it like this.
              \[ Set(y)[t/x] := Set(y[t/x]) \]
              \[ (t_0=t_1)[t/x] := (t_0[t/x] = t_1[t/x]) \]
              \[ (t_0 \in t_1)[t/x] := (t_0[t/x] \in t_1[t/x]) \]
        \item if formula $A$ is complex we define it like this.
              \[ (B \bigcirc C)[t/x] := (B[t/x] \bigcirc C[t/x]) \]
              ($\bigcirc $ can be $\to,\land ,\lor$).
              \[ (\neg B)[t/x] := (\neg B[t/x]) \]
    \end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
    \textbf{$(schema)$} $(\forall I ,\forall E ,\exists I , \exists E)$

    \begin{tabular}{|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|}
        \hline
        Rules
         &
        Terms and Conditions
        \\
        \hline
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \hypo [ ] {2} {\phi (x)}
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore \forall x \phi (x)} \by{$\forall I$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
         &
        \begin{enumerate}
            \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
            \item  $x$ is a variable.
            \item  ``$\phi (x)$'' must not be assumed.
        \end{enumerate}

        \\
        \hline
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \hypo [ ] {2} {\forall x \phi (x)}
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore \phi [t/x]} \by{$\forall E$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
         &
        \begin{enumerate}
            \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
            \item  $t$ is a term.
        \end{enumerate}

        \\
        \hline
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \hypo [ ] {2} {\phi [t/x]}
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore \exists x \phi (x)} \by{$\exists I$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}

         &

        \begin{enumerate}
            \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
            \item  $t$ is a term.
        \end{enumerate}

        \\
        \hline
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have [ ] {1} {\exists x \phi (x)}
            \open
            \have [ ] {1} {\phi (x)}
            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
            \have  [ ] {2} {\theta}
            \close
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore\theta } \by{$\exists E$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
         &
        \begin{enumerate}
            \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
            \item  $x$ is a variable.
            \item  ``$\phi (x)$'' must not be assumed in previous lines.(except ones that are closed)
            \item $x$ is not free in $\theta$
        \end{enumerate}
        \\
        \hline
    \end{tabular}
    % \printindex
    \\
    \\
    $(\forall I , \exists E)$ is the same as $(\forall I , \exists E)$ in chapter three so we have
    to prove $(\forall E , \exists I )$. so assume $t= f(x)$ so to it's easy to see
    $Func(f)$ and $x \in Dom(f)$.

    \begin{tabular}{>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}}
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have  {1} {\forall x \phi (x)}           \by{$ass$}{}
            \have  {1} {Func(f)}                          \by{$1$}{}
            \have  {1} { x \in Dom(f)}         \by{$1$}{}
            \open
            \have  {1} { (x,y) \in f}                   \by{$acp$}{}
            \have  {1} {  f(x)=y}                       \by{$def$}{}
            \have  {1} {\phi(y)}                        \by{$\forall E$}{}
            \have  {2} {\phi(f(x))}                     \by{$= E$}{}
            \close
            \have  {2} {\phi(f(x))}                    \by{$\exists E$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
         &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have  {2} {\phi(f(x))}                    \by{$ass$}{}
            \have  {1} {Func(f)}                          \by{$ass$}{}
            \have  {1} { x \in Dom(f)}         \by{$ass$}{}
            \open
            \have  {1} { (x,y) \in f}                   \by{$acp$}{}
            \have  {1} { f(x)=y}                       \by{$def$}{}
            \have  {1} {\phi(y)}                        \by{$= E$}{}
            \have  {1} {\exists x \phi (x)}           \by{$\exists I$}{}
            \close
            \have  {1} {\exists x \phi (x)}           \by{$\exists E$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \\

    \end{tabular}

\end{theorem}

\begin{Attention}
    \textbf{} in all rules and theorems you can replace all constants like $a,b,c,...$ with
    terms like $t,s,r,....$ and rewrite them.
\end{Attention}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Compatible Function}
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item  $Compatible(f,g) : \forall x \in dom(f) \cap dom(g) \quad f(x) = g(x) $.
        \item  $Compatible(F) : \forall f,g \in F \quad Compatible(f,g) $.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma} .\\
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item  $Compatible(f,g) \therefore / \therefore Func(f \cup g) $.
        \item  $Compatible(f,g) \therefore / \therefore f \upharpoonright (dom(f) \cap dom(g)) = g \upharpoonright (dom(f) \cap dom(g)) $.
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
\end{lemma}

\begin{theorem} $Compatible(F) / \therefore (1),(2),(3)  $
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item  $Func(\bigcup F) $.
        \item  $ dom(\bigcup F) = \bigcup \{ dom(f) \mid f \in F \} $.
        \item  $ \forall f \in F \quad Compatible(f,\bigcup F) $.
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
\end{theorem}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Function from set to set}  we call a function \textbf{f} from \textbf{A} to \textbf{B}
    when it's domain is  \textbf{A} and it's range is subset of \textbf{B} i.e.
    \[ (f:A \rightarrow B) : Func(f) \land Dom(f)=A \land Range(f) \subseteq B \]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Set of functions from set to set}  we denote set of all ``$f:A \rightarrow B$'' with ``$B^A$''  i.e.
    \[ B^A : \{ f \mid f :A \rightarrow B \} \]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Injective function(Relation)}
    we call a function(Relation) \textbf{f} injective when every output has at most one input i.e.
    \[ Injective(f) : \forall y ,x_1 , x_2 \Big((x_1,y) \in f \wedge (x_2,y) \in f \rightarrow x_1 = x_2 \Big). \]

    we call a function \textbf{f} injective from \textbf{A} to \textbf{B} when it's
    function from \textbf{A} to \textbf{B} and it's injective i.e.
    \[ (f:A \xrightarrow{inj} B) :  (f:A \rightarrow B) \land Injective(f) \]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
    \textbf{$(schema)$} $(Injective I, Injective E )$

    \begin{tabular}{|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|}
        \hline
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \open
            \have  [ ]{2} {   x f z }
            \have  [ ]{2} {   y f z }
            \have [ ] {2} { \vdots }
            \have [ ] {2} { x = y }
            \close
            \have [ ] {2} { \therefore  Injective(f) }     \by{$Injective I$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}

         &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have [ ] {2} {  Injective(f) }
            \hypo   [ ]{2} {   t f r }
            \hypo   [ ]{2} {   s f r }
            \have [ ] {2} {\therefore s = r }  \by{$Injective E$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \\
        \hline
    \end{tabular}
    \\
    \\
    \\
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
\end{theorem}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Surjective function}  we call a function \textbf{f} from \textbf{A} to \textbf{B}
    when it's domain is  \textbf{A} and it's range is equal to \textbf{B} i.e.
    \[ (f:A \xrightarrow[surj]{} B) :  (f:A \rightarrow B)  \land Range(f) = B \]
    or i.e.
    \[ (f:A \xrightarrow[surj]{} B) :  (f:A \rightarrow B)  \land (\forall y \in B \quad \exists x \in A \quad (x,y) \in f) \]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Bijection function}  we call a function \textbf{f} from \textbf{A} to \textbf{B}
    when it's domain is  \textbf{A} and it's range is equal to \textbf{B} i.e.
    \[ (f:A \xrightarrow[surj]{inj} B) :  (f:A \xrightarrow{inj} B)  \land (f:A \xrightarrow[surj]{} B) \]
\end{definition}

\section{Family of sets}

\begin{Attention}
    \textbf{Indexes} are objects (like numbers) allows us to give a unique "tag" to every object.
    we use $i,j,k,...$ for variable indexes and we use $m,n,l,...$ for constant indexes.

\end{Attention}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Family of sets}  assume $A: I \to  \mathcal{F}$ is a function from a nonempty set of
    indices $I$ to a family of sets $\mathcal{F}$, then we call $(Range[A])$ an indexed family of sets.
    we can write $A_i$ instead of $A(i)$ and we can write $( A_i )_{i \in I}$ instead
    of ``$A$''  and $\{ A_i \}_{i \in I}$ instead
    of ``$Range[A]$'' or ``$A[I]$''.
    \[ ( A_i )_{i \in I} :=  A \]
    and
    \[ \{ A_i \}_{i \in I} :=  A[I] \]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Intersrction and union of an family of sets}
    \[  \bigcap_{i \in I} A_i := \bigcap \{ A_i \}_{i \in I}  \]
    \[ \bigcup_{i \in I} A_i := \bigcup \{ A_i \}_{i \in I}  \]
\end{definition}

\begin{Attention}
    \textbf{}we can write $\forall i \in I  \quad P(i)$ instead of
    $\forall i (i\in I \rightarrow  P(i) )$ and we can write $\exists i \in I  \quad P(i)$
    instead of $\exists i (i\in I  \land P(i))$.

\end{Attention}

\begin{lemma}
    \[ /\therefore  \bigcap_{i \in I} A_i =  \{x \mid \forall i \in I \quad x \in A_i\} \]
    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
        \open
        \have {1} { x \in \bigcap_{i \in I} A_i  }                                 \by{$acp$}{}
        \have  {2} {x \in \bigcap \{ A_i \}_{i \in I}}                                \by{$def.1$}{}
        \have  {2} {x \in \bigcap A[I]}                                               \by{$def.1$}{}
        \have  {2} {\forall Y  \in  A[I] \quad x \in Y }                              \by{$def \bigcap$}{}
        \have  {2} {\forall Y ((\exists i \in I \quad A_i=Y) \to x \in Y) }           \by{$def image$}{}
        \have  {2} {(\exists i \in I \quad A_i=A_{i'}) \to x \in A_{i'} }              \by{$\forall E$}{}
        \open
        \have  {2} {i' \in I }                                                         \by{$acp$}{}
        \have  {2} { A_{i'}=A_{i'} }                                                   \by{$=I$}{}
        \have  {2} { i' \in I \land A_{i'}=A_{i'} }                                      \by{$\land I$}{}
        \have  {2} { \exists i \in I \quad A_i=A_{i'} }                                   \by{$\exists I$}{}
        \have {1} {  x \in A_{i'}  }                                                       \by{$\to E$}{}
        \close
        \have {1} {  i' \in I \to x \in A_{i'}  }                                            \by{$\to I$}{}
        \have {1} { \forall i \in I \quad x \in A_i  }                                    \by{$\forall I$}{}
        \have {1} { x \in \{x \mid \forall i \in I \quad x \in A_i\}  }                 \by{$def$}{}
        \close
        \open
        \have {1} { x \in \{x \mid \forall i \in I \quad x \in A_i\}  }                \by{$acp$}{}
        \have {1} { \forall i \in I \quad x \in A_i  }                                 \by{$def$}{}
        \open
        \have  {2} {\exists i \in I \quad A_i=Y}                                       \by{$acp$}{}
        \open
        \have  {2} { i \in I }                                                              \by{$acp$}{}
        \have  {2} {x \in A_i}                                                             \by{$\forall E$}{}
        \have  {2} {  A_i=Y}                                                             \by{$\land E$}{}
        \have  {2} {x \in Y}                                                             \by{$= E$}{}
        \close
        \have  {2} {x \in Y}                                                                \by{$\exists E$}{}
        \close
        \have  {2} {(\exists i \in I \quad A_i=Y) \to x \in Y }                            \by{$\to I$}{}
        \have  {2} {\forall Y ((\exists i \in I \quad A_i=Y) \to x \in Y) }              \by{$\forall I$}{}
        \have  {2} {\forall Y  \in  A[I] \quad x \in Y }                                  \by{$def$}{}
        \have  {2} {x \in \bigcap A[I]}                                                   \by{$def$}{}
        \have  {2} {x \in \bigcap \{ A_i \}_{i \in I}}                                   \by{$def$}{}
        \have {1} { x \in \bigcap_{i \in I} A_i  }                                        \by{$def$}{}
        \close
        \have  {2} { \bigcap_{i \in I} A_i =  \{x \mid \forall i \in I \quad x \in A_i\}  }  \by{$=I$}{}

    \end{fitchproof}

    \[ /\therefore \bigcup_{i \in I} A_i =  \{x \mid \exists i \in I \quad x \in A_i\}  \]

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
        \open
        \have {1} { x \in \bigcup_{i \in I} A_i  }                                 \by{$acp$}{}
        \have  {2} {x \in \bigcup \{ A_i \}_{i \in I}}                                \by{$def.1$}{}
        \have  {2} {x \in \bigcup A[I]}                                               \by{$def.1$}{}
        \have  {2} {\exists Y  \in  A[I] \quad x \in Y }                              \by{$def \bigcup$}{}
        \have  {2} {\exists Y ((\exists i \in I \quad A_i=Y) \land x \in Y) }           \by{$def.image$}{}
        \open
        \have  {2} { x \in Y }                                                          \by{$acp$}{}
        \have  {2} {\exists i \in I \quad A_i=Y }                                        \by{$acp$}{}
        \open
        \have  {2} {i \in I }                                                         \by{$acp$}{}
        \have  {2} { A_i=Y }                                                   \by{$acp$}{}
        \have  {2} { x \in A_i }                                      \by{$= E$}{}
        \have  {2} {\exists i \in I \quad x \in A_i }                                      \by{$\exists I$}{}
        \close
        \have  {2} {\exists i \in I \quad x \in A_i }                                      \by{$\exists E$}{}
        \close
        \have  {2} {\exists i \in I \quad x \in A_i }                                      \by{$\exists E$}{}
        \have {1} { x \in \{x \mid \exists i \in I \quad x \in A_i\}  }                 \by{$def$}{}
        \close
        \open
        \have {1} { x \in \{x \mid \exists i \in I \quad x \in A_i\}  }                \by{$acp$}{}
        \have {1} { \exists i \in I \quad x \in A_i  }                                 \by{$def$}{}
        \open
        \have  {2} { i' \in I }                                                        \by{$acp$}{}
        \have  {2} { x \in A_{i'} }                                                      \by{$acp$}{}
        \have  {2} {  A_{i'}=A_{i'} }                                                     \by{$= I$}{}
        \have  {2} {\exists i \in I \quad  A_i=A_{i'} }                                       \by{$\exists I$}{}
        \have  {2} {(\exists i \in I \quad  A_i=A_{i'})\land x \in A_{i'} }                    \by{$\land I$}{}
        \have  {2} {\exists Y ((\exists i \in I \quad A_i=Y) \land x \in Y) }              \by{$\exists I$}{}

        \close

        \have  {2} {\exists Y ((\exists i \in I \quad A_i=Y) \land x \in Y) }              \by{$\exists E$}{}
        \have  {2} {\exists Y  \in  A[I] \quad x \in Y }                                  \by{$def$}{}
        \have  {2} {x \in \bigcup A[I]}                                                   \by{$def$}{}
        \have  {2} {x \in \bigcup \{ A_i \}_{i \in I}}                                   \by{$def$}{}
        \have {1} { x \in \bigcup_{i \in I} A_i  }                                        \by{$def$}{}
        \close
        \have  {2} { \bigcup_{i \in I} A_i =  \{x \mid \exists i \in I \quad x \in A_i\}  }  \by{$=I$}{}

    \end{fitchproof}

\end{lemma}

\begin{theorem}
    \textbf{$(schema)$} $(\bigcap_{I} I , \bigcap_{I} E , \bigcup_{I} I , \bigcup_{I} E)$

    \begin{tabular}{|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|}
        \hline
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \open
            \have [ ] {1} {i \in I }
            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
            \have  [ ] {2} {t \in A_i}
            \close
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore t \in \bigcap_{i \in I} A_i } \by{$\bigcap_{I} I$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
         &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \hypo [ ] {2} {t \in \bigcap_{i \in I} A_i }
            \hypo [ ] {2} {n \in  I  }

        \end{fitchproof}
        \\
        \hline
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \hypo [ ] {2} {t \in A_n  }
            \hypo [ ] {2} {n \in I  }
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore  t \in \bigcup_{i \in I} A_i } \by{$\bigcup_{I} I$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
         &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have [ ] {1} { t \in \bigcup_{i \in I} A_i  }
            \open
            \have [ ] {1} { i \in I }
            \have [ ] {1} {  t \in A_i }
            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
            \have  [ ] {2} {\theta}
            \close
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore\theta } \by{$\bigcup_{I} E$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \\
        \hline

    \\
    \\
    \\
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
\end{theorem}

\section{Structures and morphisms}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Structure} ``$\mathfrak{M} $'' is a tuple defined like this.
    \[\mathfrak{M}  := (M , f_{0}, f_{1},..., f_{n},R_{0}, R_{1} ,...,R_{m}, c_{0}, c_{1},..., c_{k}) \]
    ``$M$'' is a \textbf{nonempty} set shows our universe ``$\forall i \in \{ 0 , ... , n \} \quad f_i : M^{n'} \to M$'' are funcions and ``
    $\forall i \in \{ 0 , ... , m \} \quad R_i \subseteq M^{m'}$''
    are relations and ``$\forall i \in \{ 0 , ... , k \} \quad c_i \in M  $'' are constants.
    ($M^n$ means $\overbrace{M \times M \times ... \times M}^{\text{n times}} $ we will define it later)
    and for the case we have arbitary family of functions and relations and constants.
    \[\mathfrak{M}  := (M , \{f_i \}_{i \in I_f},\{R_i \}_{i \in I_R}, \{c_i \}_{i \in I}) \]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Homorphism} if  ``$\mathfrak{M} := (M , \{f_{i,m} \}_{i \in I_f},\{R_{i,m} \}_{i \in I_R}, \{c_{i,m} \}_{i \in I}) $''
    and ``$\mathfrak{N} := (N , \{f_{i,n} \}_{i \in I_f},\{R_{i,n} \}_{i \in I_R}, \{c_{i,n} \}_{i \in I}) $'' are two structures.
    function ``$h : M \to N$''is called ``(weak) Homorphism'' when this conditions holds
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item for eveery relation ``$R_{i,m}$''
              \[ \forall x_0 , x_1 , x_2 , ... , x_k \in M \quad R_{i,m}(x_0 , x_1 , x_2 , ... , x_k) \to R_{i,n}(h(x_0),h(x_1) ,h(x_2), ... ,h(x_k)) \].
        \item for eveery function ``$f_{i,m}$''
              \[ \forall x_0 , x_1 , x_2 , ... , x_k \in M \quad h(f_{i,m}(x_0 , x_1 , x_2 , ... , x_k)) = f_{i,n}(h(x_0),h(x_1) ,h(x_2), ... ,h(x_k)) \].
        \item for eveery constant ``$c_{i,m}$''
              \[  h(c_{i,m}) = c_{i,n} \].
    \end{enumerate}
    in condition (1) if ``$\leftrightarrow$'' be instead of ``$\to $'' then it's called  ``(strong) Homorphism''
\end{definition}

few notices: when we are talking about ``Homorphism'' we usually talk about
``(strong) Homorphism'' .\\ in the case (in definition ``(strong) Homorphism'')
we denote it by ``$Hom(h,\mathfrak{M} , \mathfrak{N} )$''. if exists function
``$h$'' ``$Hom(h,\mathfrak{M} , \mathfrak{N} )$'' we say ``$\mathfrak{M}$'' and
``$\mathfrak{N}$'' are homomorphic. we denote it by ``$Hom(\mathfrak{M} ,
    \mathfrak{N} )$''.\\ if ``$h : M \xrightarrow[]{inj} N$'' we call it
``Monomorphism'' or ``Embedding''.we say ``$\mathfrak{M}$'' and
``$\mathfrak{N}$'' are momomorphic.\\ if ``$h : M \xrightarrow[surj]{} N$'' we
call it ``Epimorphism''.we say ``$\mathfrak{M}$'' and ``$\mathfrak{N}$'' are
Eomomorphic.\\ if ``$h : M \xrightarrow[surj]{inj} N$'' we call it
``Isomorphism''.we say ``$\mathfrak{M}$'' and ``$\mathfrak{N}$'' are
Isomomorphic. and denote it by ``$ \mathfrak{M} \cong \mathfrak{N}$''.\\ if
``$k : M \to M$'' is a function and ``$Hom(k,\mathfrak{M},\mathfrak{M})$''. we
call ``$k$'' ``Indomorphism''.\\ if ``$k : M \xrightarrow[surj]{inj} M$'' is a
bijection and ``$Hom(k,\mathfrak{M},\mathfrak{M})$''. we call ``$k$''
``Automorphism''.\\

\section{Equivalences and Partitions}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Equivalence relation} assume  a structure ``$\mathfrak{A} := (A , \sim ) $''(``$\sim $'' is a relation).
    we say ``$\sim $''
    is a ``Equivalence relation'' in  ``$\mathfrak{A}$'' if it's  reflexive,symmetric, and transitive.
    \[ (reflexive) :  \forall x \in A \quad x \sim x \]
    \[ (symmetric) :   \forall x,y \in A \quad x \sim y \to y \sim x \]
    \[ (transitive) :  \forall x,y,z \in A \quad x \sim y \land y \sim z \to x \sim z  \]
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma} ``$\cong$'' is a equivalence relation in ``$(Structure , \cong)$''\\
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
\end{lemma}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Equivalence class of ``$x$''} Let ``$\sim$'' be an equivalence relation on ``$\mathfrak{A}$''. For every ``$x \in A$''

    \[ [x]_{\sim} := \{y \in A \mid y \sim x \} \]

    you can also see it as a function $[\_]_{\sim} : A \to V$.

\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
    \textbf{$(schema)$} $([]_{\sim} I, []_{\sim}  E )$

    \begin{tabular}{|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|}
        \hline
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \hypo [ ] {2} { t \sim s }
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore   t \in  [s]_{\sim}  } \by{$[]_{\sim} I$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
         &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \hypo [ ] {2} { t \in  [s]_{\sim} }
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore  t \sim s  } \by{$[]_{\sim} E$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \\
        \hline
    \end{tabular}
    \\
    \\
    \\
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
\end{theorem}

\begin{lemma}
    For every ``$a,b \in A$''
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item  $a \sim b \therefore / \therefore [a]_{\sim} = [b]_{\sim}$
        \item  $a \nsim  b \therefore / \therefore [a]_{\sim} \cap [b]_{\sim} = \emptyset$
    \end{enumerate}

    prove.1 \\
    \begin{tabular}{>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}}

        $/ \therefore$

        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have  {2} { a \sim b}     \by{$ass$}{}
            \open
            \have  {2} {x \in [a]_{\sim} }   \by{$acp$}{}
            \have  {2} {x \sim a}              \by{$[]_{\sim} E$}{}
            \have  {2} {x \sim b}            \by{$trans$}{}
            \have  {2} {x \in [b]_{\sim} }      \by{$[]_{\sim} I$}{}
            \close
            \open
            \have  {2} {x \in [b]_{\sim} }         \by{$acp$}{}
            \have  {2} {x \sim b}                    \by{$[]_{\sim} E$}{}
            \have  {2} {x \sim a}                \by{$trans$}{}
            \have  {2} {x \in [a]_{\sim} }         \by{$[]_{\sim} I$}{}
            \close
            \have  {2} { [a]_{\sim} = [b]_{\sim} }
        \end{fitchproof}
         &
        $ \therefore /$
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have  {2} { [a]_{\sim} = [b]_{\sim} }      \by{$ass$}{}
            \have  {2} {a \sim a  }                     \by{$reflex$}{}
            \have  {2} {a \in [a]_{\sim} }               \by{$[]_{\sim} I$}{}
            \have  {2} {a \in [b]_{\sim} }                  \by{$= E$}{}
            \have  {2} {a \sim b  }                     \by{$[]_{\sim} E$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \\

    \end{tabular}

    prove.2 \\
    \begin{tabular}{>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}}

        $/ \therefore$
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have  {2} { a \nsim b}     \by{$ass$}{}
            \open
            \have  {2} { [a]_{\sim} \cap [b]_{\sim}  \not= \emptyset }   \by{$acp$}{}
            \open
            \have  {2} { x \in [a]_{\sim} \cap [b]_{\sim} }        \by{$acp$}{}
            \have  {2} { x \in [a]_{\sim}  }            \by{$ \cap E$}{}
            \have  {2} { x \in  [b]_{\sim} }            \by{$ \cap E$}{}
            \have  {2} {x \sim a}                     \by{$[]_{\sim} E$}{}
            \have  {2} {x \sim b}                     \by{$[]_{\sim} E$}{}
            \have  {2} {a \sim b }                   \by{$trans$}{}
            \have  {2} {\bot }
            \close
            \have  {2} {\bot }
            \close
            \have  {2} { [a]_{\sim} \cap [b]_{\sim}  = \emptyset }
        \end{fitchproof}
         &
        $ \therefore /$
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have  {2} { [a]_{\sim} \cap [b]_{\sim}  = \emptyset }
            \open
            \have  {2} {a \sim b  }           \by{$acp$}{}
            \have  {2} {a \sim a  }            \by{$reflex$}{}
            \have  {2} {a \in [a]_{\sim} }      \by{$[]_{\sim} I$}{}
            \have  {2} {a \in [b]_{\sim} }      \by{$[]_{\sim} I$}{}
            \have  {2} {a \in [a]_{\sim} \cap [b]_{\sim} }       \by{$ \cap I$}{}
            \have  {2} {\bot}
            \close
            \have  {2} {a \nsim b  }
        \end{fitchproof}
        \\

    \end{tabular}

\end{lemma}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Partition} is a ``set of sets''  like ``$P$'' of a set like  ``$A$''  with this conditions.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item  $\forall X \in P \quad X \not= \emptyset$
        \item  $\forall X,Y \in P \quad  X \not= Y \to X \cap Y = \emptyset$
        \item  $\bigcup P = A$ \\
              if these condition holds then we say ``$Part(P,A)$''.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Set of Equivalence classes}

    \[ A/\sim := \{ [x]_{\sim} \mid x \in  A \} \]

\end{definition}

\begin{theorem} if ``$\sim$'' is a equivalence class in ``$(A,\sim)$'' then
    ``$A/\sim$'' is a partition for ``$A$'' i.e. ``$Part(A/\sim,A)$''.

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
        \open
        \have  {2} {  [x]_{\sim} \in A / \sim  }  \by{$acp$}{}
        \have  {2} { x  \sim  x  }        \by{$reflex$}{}
        \have  {2} { x \in [x]_{\sim}  }       \by{$[]_{\sim} I$}{}
        \have  {2} {  [x]_{\sim} \not= \emptyset  }        \by{$\not= \emptyset I$}{}
        \close
        \have  {2} { \forall  [x]_{\sim} \in A / \sim \quad  [x]_{\sim} \not= \emptyset   }
    \end{fitchproof}

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
        \open
        \have  {2} { [x]_{\sim} \not= [y]_{\sim}  }     \by{$acp$}{}
        \have  {2} { x \nsim  y  }                       \by{$Lemma 5.6.2.1$}{}
        \have  {2} { [x]_{\sim} \cap [y]_{\sim}  = \emptyset }  \by{$Lemma 5.6.2.2$}{}
        \close
        \have  {2} { \forall  [x]_{\sim} , [y]_{\sim} \in A / \sim \quad  [x]_{\sim} \not= [y]_{\sim}  \to   [x]_{\sim} \cap [y]_{\sim}  = \emptyset }
    \end{fitchproof}

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
        \open
        \have  {2} { x \in \bigcup (A/\sim)  }           \by{$acp$}{}
        \open
        \have  {2} { [y]_{\sim} \in A/\sim   }               \by{$acp$}{}
        \have  {2} { x \in [y]_{\sim}  }                    \by{$acp$}{}
        \have  {2} { [y]_{\sim} \subseteq A  }
        \have  {2} { x \in A  }         \by{$\subseteq E$}{}
        \close
        \have  {2} { x \in A  }                \by{$\bigcup E$}{}
        \close
        \open
        \have  {2} { x \in A  }
        \have  {2} { x \in [x]_\sim   }
        \have  {2} {  [x]_\sim \in (A/\sim)  }
        \have  {2} { x \in \bigcup(A/\sim)  }         \by{$\bigcup I$}{}
        \close
        \have  {2} { A  = \bigcup(A/\sim)  }            \by{$= I$}{}
    \end{fitchproof}

\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem} if ``$P$'' is a partition for ``$A$''  we define
    \[ \sim_P := \{ (x,y) \mid \exists Y \in P \quad ( x \in  Y \land y \in  Y) \} \]
    then ``$\sim_P$'' is a equivalence class in ``$(A,\sim_P)$''.

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
        \open
        \have  {2} { x \in A  }          \by{$acp$}{}
        \have  {2} { x \in \bigcup P  }      \by{$Def$}{}
        \open
        \have  {2} {x \in Y  }             \by{$acp$}{}
        \have  {2} { Y \in P  }             \by{$acp$}{}
        \have  {2} {\exists Y \in P \quad (x \in Y \land x \in Y  )  }
        \have  {2} {x \sim_P x  }
        \close
        \have  {2} {x \sim_P x  }
        \close
        \have  {2} {\forall x \in A \quad x \sim_P x  }

    \end{fitchproof}

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
        \open
        \have  {2} { x , y \in A }      \by{$acp$}{}
        \open
        \have  {2} { x \sim_P y  }            \by{$acp$}{}
        \have  {2} {\exists Y \in P \quad ( x \in Y \land y \in Y  )  }   \by{$Def$}{}
        \open
        \have  {2} { Y \in P \land  x \in Y \land y \in Y    }      \by{$acp$}{}
        \have  {2} { Y \in P \land  y \in Y \land x \in Y    }      \by{$commutative \land$}{}
        \have  {2} {\exists Y \in P \quad ( y \in Y \land x \in Y  )  }
        \have  {2} { y \sim_P x  }
        \close
        \have  {2} { y \sim_P x  }
        \close
        \have  {2} {x \sim_P y \to  y \sim_P x  }
        \close
        \have  {2} {\forall x,y \in A \quad x \sim_P y \to  y \sim_P x }
    \end{fitchproof}

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
        \open
        \have  {2} { x , y ,z \in A }         \by{$acp$}{}
        \open
        \have  {2} { x \sim_P y  }             \by{$acp$}{}
        \have  {2} { y \sim_P z  }               \by{$acp$}{}
        \have  {2} {\exists Y \in P \quad ( x \in Y \land y \in Y  )  }
        \have  {2} {\exists Y \in P \quad ( y \in Y \land z \in Y  )  }
        \open
        \have  {2} { Y \in P \land  x \in Y \land y \in Y    }
        \open
        \have  {2} { Y' \in P \land  y \in Y' \land z \in Y'    }
        \have  {2} {Y \cap Y' \not= \emptyset  }
        \have  {2} {Y = Y'  }
        \have  {2} {Y \in P \land  x \in Y \land z \in Y   }
        \have  {2} {\exists Y \in P \quad ( x \in Y \land z \in Y  )  }
        \have  {2} { x \sim_P z  }
        \close
        \have  {2} { x \sim_P z  }
        \close
        \have  {2} { x \sim_P z  }
        \close
        \have  {2} { x \sim_P y \land y \sim_p z \to  x \sim_P z  }

        \close
        \have  {2} {\forall x,y,z \in A \quad x \sim_P y \land y \sim_p z \to  x \sim_P z }
    \end{fitchproof}

\end{theorem}

\section{Orderings}
\begin{definition}
    \textbf{(partial) Order relation} assume  a structure ``$\mathfrak{A} := (A , \preceq  ) $''(``$\preceq $'' is a relation).
    we say ``$\preceq  $''
    is a ``(partial) Order relation'' in  ``$\mathfrak{A}$'' if it's  reflexive,antisymmetric, and transitive.
    \[ (reflexive) :  \forall x \in A \quad x \preceq x \]
    \[ (antisymmetric) :   \forall x,y \in A \quad x \preceq y \land y \preceq x \to x=y \]
    \[ (transitive) :  \forall x,y,z \in A \quad x \preceq y \land y \preceq z \to x \preceq z  \]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{(partial) Strict order relation} assume  a structure ``$\mathfrak{A} := (A , \prec  ) $''(``$\prec $'' is a relation).
    we say ``$\prec  $''
    is a ``(partial) Strict order relation'' in  ``$\mathfrak{A}$'' if it's  irreflexive, and transitive.
    \[ (irreflexive) :  \forall x \in A \quad x \nprec  x \]
    \[ (transitive) :  \forall x,y,z \in A \quad x \prec y \land y \prec z \to x \prec z  \]
    by two conditions bellow we can proof asymmetric too.
    \[ (asymmetric) :   \forall x,y \in A \quad x \prec y \to y \nprec x \]

\end{definition}

now we will show you relation between ``(partial) Order relation'' and
``(partial) Strict order relation''.

\begin{theorem}.\\
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       if ``$\preceq $'' is a ``(partial) Order relation'' in ``$\mathfrak{A}$''
              define ``$a \prec b : a \preceq b \land a \not= b $'' prove that ``$\prec $''
              is ``(partial) Strict order relation''.

              \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
                  \open
                  \have  {2} { x \in A }    \by{$acp$}{}
                  \open
                  \have  {2} { x \prec x }     \by{$acp$}{}
                  \have  {2} { x \not= x }     \by{$\land E$}{}
                  \have  {2} { x = x }     \by{$= I$}{}
                  \have  {2} { \bot  }           \by{$\bot I$}{}
                  \close
                  \have  {2} { x \nprec x }             \by{$\neg I$}{}
                  \close
                  \have  {2} {\forall x \in A \quad x \nprec x }  \by{$\to I , \forall I $}{}
              \end{fitchproof}

              \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
                  \open
                  \have  {2} { x,y,z \in A }      \by{$acp$}{}
                  \open
                  \have  {2} { x \prec y }       \by{$acp$}{}
                  \have  {2} { y \prec z }        \by{$acp$}{}
                  \have  {2} { x \preceq y }   \by{$ \land E$}{}
                  \have  {2} { y \preceq z }    \by{$\land E$}{}
                  \have  {2} { x \preceq z } \by{$trans$}{}
                  \have  {2} { x \not= y }    \by{$\land E$}{}
                  \have  {2} { y \not= z }     \by{$\land E$}{}
                  \open
                  \have  {2} { x = z }     \by{$acp$}{}
                  \have  {2} { y \preceq x }  \by{$= E$}{}
                  \have  {2} { x = y }        \by{$antisym$}{}
                  \have  {2} { \bot  }       \by{$\bot I$}{}
                  \close
                  \have  {2} { x \not= z }       \by{$\neg I$}{}
                  \have  {2} { x \prec z }        \by{$\land I$}{}
                  \close
                  \have  {2} { x \prec y \land y \prec z \to x \prec z }        \by{$\to I$}{}
                  \close
                  \have  {2} {\forall x,y,z \in A \quad x \prec y \land y \prec z \to x \prec z }   \by{$\to I, \forall I $}{}

              \end{fitchproof}

        \item       if ``$\prec $'' is a ``(partial) Strict Order relation'' in ``$\mathfrak{A}$''
              define ``$a \preceq b : a \prec b \lor a = b $'' prove that ``$\preceq $'' is
              ``(partial) order relation''.

              \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
                  \open
                  \have  {2} { x \in A }   \by{$acp$}{}
                  \have  {2} { x = x }          \by{$= I$}{}
                  \have  {2} { x \preceq x }     \by{$\lor I$}{}
                  \close
                  \have  {2} {\forall x \in A \quad x \preceq x }      \by{$\to I, \forall I $}{}
              \end{fitchproof}

              \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
                  \open
                  \have  {2} { x,y \in A }      \by{$acp$}{}
                  \open
                  \have  {2} { x \preceq y \land y \preceq x }     \by{$acp$}{}
                  \open
                  \have  {2} { x \prec y \land y \prec x }     \by{$acp$}{}
                  \have  {2} { \bot  }                          \by{$asym$}{}
                  \have  {2} { x = y  }
                  \close
                  \open
                  \have  {2} { x \prec y \land y = x }         \by{$acp$}{}
                  \have  {2} { x = y  }
                  \close
                  \open
                  \have  {2} { x = y \land y \prec x }          \by{$acp$}{}
                  \have  {2} { x = y  }
                  \close
                  \open
                  \have  {2} { x = y \land y = x }           \by{$acp$}{}
                  \have  {2} { x = y  }
                  \close
                  \have  {2} { x = y  }                      \by{$\lor E$}{}
                  \close
                  \have  {2} { x \preceq y \land y \preceq x \to x=y }      \by{$\to I$}{}
                  \close
                  \have  {2} {\forall x,y \in A \quad x \preceq y \land y \preceq x \to x=y }      \by{$\to I, \forall I $}{}
              \end{fitchproof}

              \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
                  \open
                  \have  {2} { x,y,z \in A }     \by{$acp$}{}
                  \open
                  \have  {2} { x \preceq y \land y \preceq z }      \by{$acp$}{}
                  \open
                  \have  {2} { x \prec y \land y \prec z }          \by{$acp$}{}
                  \have  {2} { x \prec z }                           \by{$trans$}{}
                  \have  {2} { x \preceq z }                         \by{$\lor I$}{}
                  \close
                  \open
                  \have  {2} { x \prec y \land y = z }        \by{$acp$}{}
                  \have  {2} { x \prec z }                     \by{$= E$}{}
                  \have  {2} { x \preceq z }                   \by{$\lor I$}{}
                  \close
                  \open
                  \have  {2} { x = y \land y \prec z }             \by{$acp$}{}
                  \have  {2} { x \prec z }                           \by{$= E$}{}
                  \have  {2} { x \preceq z }                           \by{$\lor I$}{}
                  \close
                  \open
                  \have  {2} { x = y \land y = z }          \by{$acp$}{}
                  \have  {2} { x = z }                        \by{$= E$}{}
                  \have  {2} { x \preceq z }                   \by{$\lor I$}{}
                  \close
                  \have  {2} {x \preceq z  }                   \by{$\lor E$}{}
                  \close
                  \have  {2} { x \preceq y \land y \prec z \to x \preceq z }           \by{$\to I$}{}
                  \close
                  \have  {2} {\forall x,y,z \in A \quad x \preceq y \land y \prec z \to x \preceq z } \by{$\to I, \forall I $}{}
              \end{fitchproof}

    \end{enumerate}

\end{theorem}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{(Total-Linear) Order relation} assume  a structure ``$\mathfrak{A} := (A , \preceq  ) $''(``$\preceq $'' is a Order relation).
    we say ``$\preceq  $''
    is a ``(Total-Linear) Order relation'' in  ``$\mathfrak{A}$'' if every two elements are comparable.

    \[ (comparablity) :   \forall x,y \in A \quad x \preceq y \lor y \preceq x  \]

\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{(Total-Linear) Strict order relation} assume  a structure ``$\mathfrak{A} := (A , \prec  ) $''(``$\prec $'' is a Strict order relation).
    we say ``$\prec  $''
    is a ``(Total-Linear) Strict order relation'' in  ``$\mathfrak{A}$'' if every two elements are comparable.

    \[ (comparablity) :   \forall x,y \in A \quad x \prec y \lor x=y \lor y \prec x \]

\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}.\\
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       if ``$\preceq $'' is a ``(Total-Linear) Order relation'' in ``$\mathfrak{A}$''
              define ``$a \prec b : a \preceq b \land a \not= b $'' prove that ``$\prec $''
              is ``(Total-Linear) Strict order relation''. \\ Proof Is Left As An Exercise To
              The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
        \item       if ``$\prec $'' is a ``(Total-Linear) Strict Order relation'' in
              ``$\mathfrak{A}$'' define ``$a \preceq b : a \prec b \lor a = b $'' prove that
              ``$\preceq $'' is ``(Total-Linear) order relation''. \\ Proof Is Left As An
              Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
    \end{enumerate}

\end{theorem}

\begin{lemma}
    assume  a structure ``$\mathfrak{A} := (A , \preceq  ) $''(``$\preceq $'' is a Order relation).
    and $ B,C \subseteq A $ prove
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $\exists b \in B \quad \forall x \in B \quad b \preceq x /
                  \therefore \exists ! b \in B \quad \forall x \in B \quad b \preceq x $
              \\ Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}.
              in this case we say ``$B$'' has  least element or Minimum or ``$HaveLeast(B)$''
        \item       $\exists c \in C \quad \forall x \in C \quad x \preceq c /
                  \therefore \exists ! c \in C \quad \forall x \in C \quad x \preceq c $
              \\ Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}.
              in this case we say ``$C$'' has  greatest element or has Maximum  or ``$HaveGreatest(C)$''

    \end{enumerate}

\end{lemma}

\begin{theorem}
    \textbf{Minimum and Maximum} assume  a structure ``$\mathfrak{A} := (A , \preceq  ) $''(``$\preceq $'' is a Order relation).
    and $\mathcal{B} := \{ B \subseteq A \mid  HaveLeast(B) \}$ and
    $ \mathcal{C} := \{ C \subseteq A \mid  HaveGreatest(C)  \}$
    we define  like this
    \[ min_\mathfrak{A} :=  \{ (B,b) \in \mathcal{B} \times V \mid \forall x \in B \quad b \preceq x \}  \]
    \[ max_\mathfrak{A} := \{ (C,c) \in \mathcal{C} \times V \mid \forall x \in C \quad x \preceq c \}  \]
    prove these two relations are two functions ``$min_\mathfrak{A} : \mathcal{B}
        \to V$'' and ``$max_\mathfrak{A} : \mathcal{C} \to V$''.\twemoji{1f600}
\end{theorem}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Minimal and Maximal} assume  a structure ``$\mathfrak{A} := (A , \preceq  ) $''(``$\preceq $'' is a Order relation).
    we define ``Minimal and Maximal'' like this
    \[ Minimal_\mathfrak{A}(b) :   \forall x \in A \quad x \nprec   b  \]
    i.e. ``$b$'' is the minimal in ``$ \mathfrak{A}$''.
    \[ Maximal_\mathfrak{A}(c) :   \forall x \in A \quad c \nprec    x \]
    i.e. ``$c$'' is the maximal in ``$ \mathfrak{A}$''.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Well ordering} assume  a structure ``$\mathfrak{A} := (A , \preceq  ) $''(``$\preceq $'' is a Order relation).
    we say ``$\mathfrak{A} $ '' is ``Well ordered'' when every nonempty subset of ``$A $ '' has a ``Minimum '' i.e.
    \[ WellOrdered(\mathfrak{A}) :   \forall X \subseteq A \quad X \not= \emptyset \to  HaveLeast(X)  \]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Lower and Upper Bound} assume  a structure ``$\mathfrak{A} := (A , \preceq  ) $''(``$\preceq $'' is a Order relation).
    $ B,C \subseteq A $. we define ``Lower and Upper Bound'' like this
    \[ Lowerb_\mathfrak{A}(b,B) :   \forall x \in B \quad b \preceq   x  \]
    i.e. ``$b$'' is the lowerbound of $B$ in ``$ \mathfrak{A}$''.
    \[ Upperb_\mathfrak{A}(c,C) :   \forall x \in C \quad x \preceq   c \]
    i.e. ``$C$'' is the upperbound of $B$ in ``$ \mathfrak{A}$''.
\end{definition}

if the stucture clear to us we don't write stucture index ``$min(\_),
    Minimal(\_) , Lowerb(\_,\_)$''. and we don't mension it.

\begin{theorem}
    \textbf{Infimum and Supremum} assume  a structure ``$\mathfrak{A} := (A , \preceq  ) $''(``$\preceq $'' is a Order relation).
    and $\mathcal{B} := \{ B \subseteq A \mid HaveGreatest \{ b \in A \mid Lowerb_\mathfrak{A}(b,B) \} \}$ and
    $ \mathcal{C} := \{ C \subseteq A \mid HaveLeast \{ c \in A \mid Upperb_\mathfrak{A}(c,C) \}  \}$
    we define ``$inf_\mathfrak{A} : \mathcal{B}
        \to V$'' and ``$sup_\mathfrak{A} : \mathcal{C} \to V$'' like this
    \[ inf_\mathfrak{A}(B) :=  max_\mathfrak{A}\{ b \in A \mid Lowerb_\mathfrak{A}(b,B) \}   \]
    \[ sup_\mathfrak{A}(B) :=  min_\mathfrak{A}\{ c \in A \mid Upperb_\mathfrak{A}(c,C) \}  \]
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}
    assume  a structure ``$\mathfrak{A} := (A , \preceq  ) $''(``$\preceq $'' is a Order relation).
    and $ B \subseteq A $ prove
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $ min_\mathfrak{A}(B) = b /
                  \therefore inf_\mathfrak{A}(B) = b $.
              \\ Proof Is Left As An Exercise ToThe Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
        \item       $  inf_\mathfrak{A}(B) = b , b \in B /
                  \therefore  min_\mathfrak{A}(B) = b $.
              \\ Proof Is Left As An Exercise ToThe Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
    \end{enumerate}
    this theorem holds for ``$max , sup$'' too.
\end{theorem}

\chapter{ The beginning of Abstract Algebra }

we want to introduce some functions ``$\bigcirc :\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}
    \to \mathbb{N}$'' to you. we use ``$m\bigcirc n$'' instead of ``$\bigcirc
    (m,n)$''.
\section{Group theory}

\begin{definition}
    A structure $\mathfrak{G} := (G , \cdot) $
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       If $\forall x ,y ,z \in G \quad (x y ) z = x (y z ) $ (assosiative $\times$),
              $\mathfrak{G}$ is a \textbf{semigroup}.
        \item       If $\mathfrak{G}$ is a \textbf{semigroup} and $\forall x \in G \quad \exists y
                  \in G \quad x y = y x = x$ (has identity $\times$), $\mathfrak{G}$ is a
              \textbf{monoid}. you can prove for every \textbf{monoid} $\mathfrak{G}$
              identity element is unique we denote it by ``$e$''(in $\times$ with ``$1$'' and
              in $+$ with $0$).
        \item       If $\mathfrak{G}$ is a \textbf{monoid} and $\forall x \in G \quad \exists y \in
                  G \quad x y = y x = e$ (has inverse $\times$), $\mathfrak{G}$ is a group.
        \item       If $\mathfrak{G}$ is a \textbf{semigroup/monoid} and $\forall x , y , z \in N
                  \quad x z = y z \to x = y $ (cancellative $\times$), $\mathfrak{G}$ is a
              \textbf{cancellative semigroup/monoid}. (you can prove every group is
              cancellative as an exercise.\twemoji{1f600})
        \item       If $\mathfrak{G}$ is a \textbf{semigroup/monoid/group} and $\forall x ,y \in G
                  \quad x y = y x $ (commutative(abelian) $\times$), $\mathfrak{G}$ is a
              \textbf{commutative(abelian) semigroup/monoid/group}.

    \end{enumerate}

\end{definition}

\section{Ring theory}

\begin{definition}
    A structure $\mathfrak{R} := (R , + ,  \cdot) $
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       If $(R , + )$ is a abelian \textbf{monoid/group} and $\forall x ,y ,z \in R
                  \quad (x y ) z = x (y z ) $ (assosiative $\times$), $\forall x ,y ,z \in R
                  \quad \Big(x (y + z ) = x y + x z \Big) \land \Big((x + y ) z = x z + y z \Big)
              $ (distributive $\times$), $\mathfrak{R}$ is a \textbf{Semiring/Ring}.
        \item       If $\mathfrak{R}$ is a \textbf{Semiring/Ring} and $\forall x \in R \quad
                  \exists y \in R \quad x y = y x = x$ (has identity $\times$), $\mathfrak{R}$ is
              a \textbf{Semiring/Ring with identity}. you can prove for every
              \textbf{Semiring/Ring with identity} $\mathfrak{R}$ identity element is unique
              we denote it by ``$1$''.
        \item       If $\mathfrak{R}$ is a \textbf{Semiring/Ring} and $\forall x ,y \in R \quad x y
                  = 0 \to x = 0 \lor y = 0 $ (zero divisor free $\times$), $\mathfrak{R}$ is a
              \textbf{zero divisor free Semiring/Ring}. (you can prove every \textbf{division
                  Semiring/Ring} is zero divisor free as an exercise.\twemoji{1f600})
        \item       If $\mathfrak{R}$ is a \textbf{Semiring/Ring with identity} and $\forall x \in
                  R \quad \exists y \in R \quad x y = y x = 1$ (has inverse $\times$),
              $\mathfrak{R}$ is a \textbf{division Semiring/Ring} with.
        \item       If $\mathfrak{R}$ is a \textbf{Semiring/Ring} and $\forall x ,y \in R \quad x y
                  = y x $ (commutative(abelian) $\times$), $\mathfrak{R}$ is a
              \textbf{commutative(abelian) Semiring/Ring}.
        \item       If $\mathfrak{R}$ is a \textbf{zero divisor free Semiring/Ring} and
              \textbf{commutative(abelian) Semiring/Ring}. $\mathfrak{R}$ is a \textbf{
                  Semidomain/Domain }.
        \item       If $\mathfrak{R}$ is a \textbf{division Semiring/Ring} and
              \textbf{commutative(abelian) Semiring/Ring}. $\mathfrak{R}$ is a \textbf{
                  SemiField/Field }.

    \end{enumerate}

\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Universal Ring Completion of a Semiring]
    (Standard name: “Grothendieck Ring Completion Theorem for Commutative Semirings”)
    Let $S$ be a (not necessarily commutative) semiring.
    There exists a ring $R$ together with a semiring homomorphism
    \[
        \eta : S \longrightarrow R
    \]
    which is universal among semiring-to-ring homomorphisms (i.e.\ every
    homomorphism $f:S\to T$ into a ring $T$ factors uniquely through $\eta$) if and
    only if the additive monoid $(S,+,0)$ is a \emph{cancellative commutative
        monoid}.

    In this case, $R$ is the Grothendieck group $G(S)$ of the additive monoid of
    $S$, with multiplication extending that of $S$ by
    \[
        [(a,b)] \cdot [(c,d)]
        =
        [(ac+bd,\; ad+bc)].
    \]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}

    We divide the proof into two parts.

    %-----------------------------------------------------------
    \subsection{(1) Necessity of cancellativity and commutativity of $+$}

    Suppose $\eta:S\to R$ is an embedding of $S$ as a subsemiring of a ring $R$.
    The additive structure of a ring is an abelian group. Thus the image $\eta(S)$
    must be closed under an abelian addition. Therefore $(S,+)$ must already be a
    commutative monoid.

    If $a+c=b+c$ in $S$, then in $R$ we have
    \[
        \eta(a)+\eta(c) = \eta(b)+\eta(c).
    \]
    Cancelling $\eta(c)$ in the ring gives $\eta(a)=\eta(b)$, hence $a=b$ since
    $\eta$ is injective. Thus $(S,+)$ must be cancellative.

    This proves necessity.

    %-----------------------------------------------------------
    \subsection{(2) Construction of the universal ring when $(S,+)$ is cancellative and commutative}

    Assume now that $(S,+)$ is a cancellative commutative monoid. We form its
    Grothendieck group:
    \[
        G(S) = (S\times S)/\!\sim,
    \]
    where the relation is
    \[
        (a,b)\sim(a',b')
        \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
        a + b' = a' + b.
    \]

    %-----------------------------------------------------------
    \subsubsection{Equivalence relation}

    Reflexivity and symmetry are immediate. For transitivity, assume
    \[
        a+b' = a'+b,
        \qquad
        a'+b'' = a''+b'.
    \]
    Add $b$ to the second equation and $b''$ to the first, and use commutativity
    and cancellativity to deduce
    \[
        a+b'' = a''+b.
    \]
    Thus $\sim$ is an equivalence relation.

    %-----------------------------------------------------------
    \subsubsection{Well-defined addition}

    Define
    \[
        [(a,b)] + [(c,d)] := [(a+c,\; b+d)].
    \]
    If $(a,b)\sim(a',b')$ and $(c,d)\sim(c',d')$, then
    \[
        a+b' = a'+b,
        \qquad
        c+d' = c'+d.
    \]
    Compute:
    \[
        (a+c) + (b'+d')
        = (a+b') + (c+d')
        = (a'+b) + (c'+d)
        = (a'+c') + (b+d).
    \]
    Thus $(a+c,b+d)\sim(a'+c',b'+d')$, so addition is well defined.

    %-----------------------------------------------------------
    \subsubsection{Well-defined multiplication}

    Define
    \[
        [(a,b)]\cdot[(c,d)]
        := [(ac+bd,\; ad+bc)].
    \]

    Assume $(a,b)\sim(a',b')$ and $(c,d)\sim(c',d')$, so
    \[
        a+b' = a'+b,
        \qquad
        c+d' = c'+d.
    \]
    Expanding distributively:
    \[
        (ac+bd) + (a'd' + b'c')
        = (a+b')(c+d')
        = (a'+b)(c'+d)
        = (a'c'+b'd') + (ad+bc).
    \]
    Thus multiplication is well defined.

    %-----------------------------------------------------------
    \subsubsection{Ring structure}

    The additive structure is an abelian group by construction. Multiplication is
    associative and distributive because it is defined via bilinearity inherited
    from $S$. The multiplicative identity is $[(1,0)]$.

    Thus $G(S)$ is a ring.

    %-----------------------------------------------------------
    \subsection{(3) Universality}

    Given any semiring-to-ring homomorphism $f:S\to T$, define
    \[
        \overline{f}:\; G(S)\to T,
        \qquad
        \overline{f}([(a,b)]) := f(a)-f(b).
    \]

    If $(a,b)\sim(a',b')$, then $a+b'=a'+b$, so applying $f$ gives
    \[
        f(a)+f(b') = f(a')+f(b),
    \]
    hence $f(a)-f(b)=f(a')-f(b')$. Thus $\overline{f}$ is well defined. It is
    easily checked to be a ring homomorphism, and satisfies $\overline{f}\circ\eta
        = f$. Uniqueness follows since $[(a,b)]=[(a,0)]-[(b,0)]$.

    This proves the universal property.

    \section{The Field of Fractions}

    The field of fractions is a fundamental concept in abstract algebra that allows
    us to extend a given integral domain to a field. It is the smallest field
    containing the integral domain.

    \subsection{Integral Domain}
    An integral domain is a commutative ring with a multiplicative identity ($1 \ne
        0$) and no zero divisors. That is, if $a \cdot b = 0$, then either $a = 0$ or
    $b = 0$. The set of integers, $\mathbb{Z}$, is a classic example.

    \subsection{Field}
    A field is a commutative ring with a multiplicative identity where every
    non-zero element has a multiplicative inverse. For every $a \ne 0$, there
    exists $b$ such that $a \cdot b = 1$. The set of rational numbers,
    $\mathbb{Q}$, is a field.

    \subsection{Construction of the Field of Fractions}
    Given an integral domain $D$, its field of fractions, denoted by $F(D)$, is
    constructed by formally creating fractions of elements from $D$. We consider
    pairs $(a, b)$ where $a, b \in D$ and $b \ne 0$. An equivalence relation $\sim$
    is defined on these pairs: $$ (a, b) \sim (c, d) \iff ad - bc = 0 $$ The
    elements of $F(D)$ are the equivalence classes of these pairs, denoted by
    $\frac{a}{b}$.

    \textbf{Operations in $F(D)$:} For $\frac{a}{b}, \frac{c}{d} \in F(D)$:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Addition: $\frac{a}{b} + \frac{c}{d} = \frac{ad + bc}{bd}$
        \item Multiplication: $\frac{a}{b} \cdot \frac{c}{d} = \frac{ac}{bd}$
    \end{itemize}

    The original integral domain $D$ is embedded into $F(D)$ via the map $\phi: D
        \to F(D)$ defined by $\phi(a) = \frac{a}{1}$.

\end{proof}

\section{Theorem: Existence and Uniqueness of the Field of Fractions}

\begin{theorem}
    Let $D$ be an integral domain. Then there exists a field $F$ and an injective ring homomorphism $\phi: D \to F$ such that for every field $K$ and every ring homomorphism $\psi: D \to K$, there exists a unique field homomorphism $f: F \to K$ such that $\psi = f \circ \phi$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    Let $D$ be an integral domain.

    \subsection*{Part 1: Existence}
    Consider the set $S = \{(a, b) \mid a, b \in D, b \ne 0\}$. Define an
    equivalence relation $\sim$ on $S$: $$ (a, b) \sim (c, d) \iff ad - bc = 0 $$
    The relation is reflexive, symmetric, and transitive because $D$ is an integral
    domain. Let $F(D)$ be the set of equivalence classes of $S$ under $\sim$. We
    denote the equivalence class of $(a, b)$ by $\frac{a}{b}$.

    \textbf{Operations:}
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Addition: $\frac{a}{b} + \frac{c}{d} = \frac{ad + bc}{bd}$
        \item Multiplication: $\frac{a}{b} \cdot \frac{c}{d} = \frac{ac}{bd}$
    \end{itemize}
    These operations are well-defined. For example, for addition, if $\frac{a}{b} = \frac{a'}{b'}$ and $\frac{c}{d} = \frac{c'}{d'}$, then $ab' = ba'$ and $cd' = dc'$. We must show $\frac{ad+bc}{bd} = \frac{a'd'+b'c'}{b'd'}$, which means showing $(ad+bc)b'd' = (a'd'+b'c')bd$. This can be verified algebraically using the properties of $D$. Similarly for multiplication.

    \textbf{Field Axioms:}
    \begin{itemize}
        \item $F(D)$ is closed under addition and multiplication.
        \item Addition and multiplication are associative, commutative, and distributive
              because these properties hold in $D$.
        \item The additive identity is $\frac{0}{1}$: $\frac{a}{b} + \frac{0}{1} = \frac{a
                      \cdot 1 + b \cdot 0}{b \cdot 1} = \frac{a}{b}$.
        \item The multiplicative identity is $\frac{1}{1}$: $\frac{a}{b} \cdot \frac{1}{1} =
                  \frac{a \cdot 1}{b \cdot 1} = \frac{a}{b}$.
        \item The additive inverse of $\frac{a}{b}$ is $\frac{-a}{b}$: $\frac{a}{b} +
                  \frac{-a}{b} = \frac{0}{b} = \frac{0}{1}$.
        \item The multiplicative inverse of a non-zero element $\frac{a}{b}$ (where $a \ne
                  0$) is $\frac{b}{a}$: $\frac{a}{b} \cdot \frac{b}{a} = \frac{ab}{ba} =
                  \frac{1}{1}$.
    \end{itemize}
    Thus, $F(D)$ is a field.

    \textbf{Injective Ring Homomorphism $\phi: D \to F(D)$:}
    Define $\phi(a) = \frac{a}{1}$.
    \begin{itemize}
        \item $\phi(a+b) = \frac{a+b}{1} = \frac{a}{1} + \frac{b}{1} = \phi(a) + \phi(b)$.
        \item $\phi(ab) = \frac{ab}{1} = \frac{a}{1} \cdot \frac{b}{1} = \phi(a) \cdot \phi(b)$.
        \item $\phi$ is injective: If $\phi(a) = \phi(b)$, then $\frac{a}{1} = \frac{b}{1}$, which implies $a \cdot 1 = b \cdot 1$, so $a=b$.
    \end{itemize}
    This establishes the existence of $F(D)$ and $\phi$.

    \subsection*{Part 2: Uniqueness}
    Suppose $K$ is another field and $\psi: D \to K$ is an injective ring
    homomorphism satisfying the universal property. We need to define a unique
    field homomorphism $f: F(D) \to K$ such that $\psi = f \circ \phi$.

    \textbf{Define $f$:} For any $\frac{a}{b} \in F(D)$, define
    $$ f\left(\frac{a}{b}\right) = \psi(a) (\psi(b))^{-1} $$
    Since $\psi$ is injective and $b \ne 0$, $\psi(b) \ne 0$, so $(\psi(b))^{-1}$ exists in $K$. This map $f$ is well-defined, as shown in the detailed explanation (if $\frac{a}{b} = \frac{c}{d}$, then $ad=bc$, and applying $\psi$ gives $\psi(a)\psi(d)=\psi(b)\psi(c)$, leading to $\psi(a)(\psi(b))^{-1} = \psi(c)(\psi(d))^{-1}$).

    \textbf{$f$ is a Field Homomorphism:}
    \begin{itemize}
        \item $f\left(\frac{a}{b} + \frac{c}{d}\right) = f\left(\frac{ad+bc}{bd}\right) = \psi(ad+bc)(\psi(bd))^{-1} = (\psi(a)\psi(d)+\psi(b)\psi(c))(\psi(b)\psi(d))^{-1} = \psi(a)(\psi(b))^{-1} + \psi(c)(\psi(d))^{-1} = f\left(\frac{a}{b}\right) + f\left(\frac{c}{d}\right)$.
        \item $f\left(\frac{a}{b} \cdot \frac{c}{d}\right) = f\left(\frac{ac}{bd}\right) = \psi(ac)(\psi(bd))^{-1} = (\psi(a)\psi(c))(\psi(b)\psi(d))^{-1} = (\psi(a)(\psi(b))^{-1}) (\psi(c)(\psi(d))^{-1}) = f\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)f\left(\frac{c}{d}\right)$.
    \end{itemize}

    \textbf{Check Composition:}
    For $a \in D$, $(f \circ \phi)(a) = f(\phi(a)) = f(\frac{a}{1}) = \psi(a)(\psi(1))^{-1} = \psi(a) \cdot 1_K^{-1} = \psi(a)$. So $\psi = f \circ \phi$.

    \textbf{Uniqueness of $f$:}
    Suppose $g: F(D) \to K$ is another field homomorphism such that $\psi = g \circ \phi$.
    For any $\frac{a}{b} \in F(D)$, we have $\frac{a}{b} = \frac{a}{1} \cdot (\frac{b}{1})^{-1}$ in $F(D)$.
    Then $g(\frac{a}{b}) = g(\frac{a}{1} \cdot (\frac{b}{1})^{-1}) = g(\frac{a}{1}) \cdot g((\frac{b}{1})^{-1}) = g(\frac{a}{1}) \cdot (g(\frac{b}{1}))^{-1}$.
    Since $\psi = g \circ \phi$, we have $g(\frac{a}{1}) = \psi(a)$ and $g(\frac{b}{1}) = \psi(b)$.
    So, $g(\frac{a}{b}) = \psi(a) (\psi(b))^{-1}$, which is precisely $f(\frac{a}{b})$.
    Thus, $f = g$, and $f$ is unique.
\end{proof}

\chapter{ Natural numbers  }

\section{Introduction to Natural numbers}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Natural numbers} we define ``$0,1,2,3,...$'' like this.
    \begin{align*}
         &
        0 := \emptyset
        \\
         &
        1 :=\{0\}= \emptyset \cup \{ \emptyset \} = \{ \emptyset \}
        \\
         &
        2 := \{0,1\}=\{ \emptyset \} \cup \big\{ \{ \emptyset \} \big\} = \big\{ \emptyset , \{ \emptyset \} \big\}
        \\
         &
        3 := \{0,1,2\}=\Big\{ \emptyset , \{ \emptyset \},\big\{ \emptyset , \{ \emptyset \} \big\} \Big\} \\
         & \vdots
    \end{align*}

\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Successor function} is a function ``$s :V \to V$'' defined like this.
    \[s(x) := x\cup \{x\}\]
\end{definition}

you can see $\forall x \quad Type_0(x) \to Type_0(s(x))$

\begin{definition}
    we define a set  \textbf{``Inductive''} like this.

    \[
        Ind(X) :  \emptyset\in X \wedge \forall y(y\in X\to s(y) \in X)
    \]

\end{definition}

notice that ``$Ind(X)$'' might be empty.

\begin{axiom}
    \textbf{The Axiom of Infinity} we define a set ``Inductive'' like this.

    \[
        \exists X \quad Type_0(X) \land Ind(X)
    \]

\end{axiom}

so ``$Ind(X)$'' is a nonempty set. so we can define

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Set of Natural numbers($\mathbb{N}$)}

    \[ \mathbb{N} := \bigcap Ind \]

\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
    \textbf{}  prove.

    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $ \mathbb{N} = \{ x  \mid \underbrace{\forall Y \Big( \big( \underbrace{\emptyset \in Y \wedge \forall z (z \in Y \to s(z)\in Y)}_{Ind(Y)} \big) \to x \in Y \Big)}_{\phi(x)} \} $
        \item       $ Ind(\mathbb{N}) $\\
              Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
    \end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

notice that now ``$\phi(x)$'' is a proposition. so ``$\{ x \mid \forall Y \Big(
    \big( \emptyset \in Y \wedge \forall z (z \in Y \to s(z)\in Y) \big) \to x \in
    Y \Big) \}$'' is a set.

\section{Properties of Natural numbers}

\begin{theorem}\textbf{Induction}
    $P(0) , \forall n \in \mathbb{N} \quad \Big( P(n) \to P(s(n)) \Big) /\therefore \forall n\in \mathbb{N} \quad P(n)$
    \\
    proof.

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
        \have {1} { S := \{ x \in \mathbb{N} \mid P(x) \}  }              \by{$ def $}{}
        \have {1} { P(0)  }     \by{$ass$}{}
        \have  {2} {\forall n \in \mathbb{N} \quad \Big( P(n) \to P(s(n)) \Big)}      \by{$ass $}{}
        \open
        \have  {2} {n\in \mathbb{N} }                                   \by{$acp $}{}
        \have  {2} { 0 \in S }                                             \by{$ 1 , 2 $}{}
        \open
        \have  {2} {n \in S  }      \by{$ acp $}{}
        \have  {2} { P(n) }                       \by{$ comprhension $}{}
        \have  {2} { P(n) \to P(s(n)) }      \by{$ \forall E , \to E , 3 , 4 $}{}
        \have  {2} { P(s(n)) }                              \by{$  \to E , 7 ,8  $}{}
        \have  {2} { s(n) \in S  }      \by{$ 4 , 9 $}{}
        \close
        \have  {2} {\forall n  \Big( n \in S  \to s(n) \in S  \Big)}      \by{$ 1 , 3 $}{}
        \have  {2} {Ind(S) }                                                 \by{$ 5 , 6 $}{}
        \have  {2} {n\in \bigcap Ind }                                        \by{$ def $}{}
        \have  {2} { n \in S }                                                \by{$ \bigcap E $}{}
        \have  {2} { P(n) }                                                   \by{$ comprhension $}{}
        \close
        \have  {2} {\forall n\in \mathbb{N} \quad P(n) }     \by{$\to I , \forall I $}{}

        \close

    \end{fitchproof}
\end{theorem}

\begin{definition}
    A set ``$T$'' is  \textbf{Transitive} if $u \in v \in T$ implies $ u \in T$.

    \[
        Transitive(T) : \forall u,v \quad  u \in v \in T \to u \in T
    \]
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}.\\
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $\forall n \in \mathbb{N} \quad  Transitive(n)  $
        \item       $Transitive( \mathbb{N})$
    \end{enumerate}

    proof.
    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

        \have  {2} { \forall v \quad  v \notin 0    }                           \by{$ $}{}
        \have  {2} { \forall u,v \quad u \in v \in 0  \to u \in 0  }                           \by{$ $}{}
        \have  {2} {  Transitive(0) }                           \by{$ $}{}
        \open
        \have  {2} {n\in \mathbb{N} }                                   \by{$acp $}{}
        \open
        \have  {2} {  Transitive(n) }                          \by{$acp $}{}
        \open
        \have  {2} { u \in v \land v \in s(n)    }                            \by{$acp $}{}
        \have  {2} {  v \in n \cup \{ n \}    }                           \by{$ \land E $}{}
        \open
        \have  {2} {  v \in n    }                           \by{$ \land E $}{}
        \have  {2} { u \in n   }                            \by{$5 , 6 ,8 $}{}
        \have  {2} {  u \in s(n)  }                           \by{$ \cup I $}{}
        \close
        \open
        \have  {2} {  v \in \{ n \}    }                           \by{$ \land E $}{}
        \have  {2} {  v = n    }                           \by{$  $}{}
        \have  {2} { u \in n   }                            \by{$ = E $}{}
        \have  {2} {  u \in s(n)  }                           \by{$ \cup I $}{}
        \close
        \have  {2} {  u \in s(n)  }                           \by{$ $}{}
        \close
        \have  {2} { \forall u,v \quad u \in v \in s(n)  \to u \in s(n)  }                           \by{$ $}{}
        \have  {2} {  Transitive(s(n)) }                           \by{$ $}{}
        \close
        \close
        \have  {2} {\forall n \in \mathbb{N} \quad  Transitive(n) \to Transitive(s(n)) }
        \have  {2} {\forall n \in \mathbb{N} \quad  Transitive(n) }                                   \by{ Induction theorem }{}

    \end{fitchproof}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
\end{lemma}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Order in Natural numbers($\mathbb{N}$)}

    \[
        < := \{ (x,y) \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}  \mid x \in y \}
    \]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
    \textbf{Order of Natural numbers($\mathbb{N}$)} prove
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item        $ (\mathbb{N} , <  )  $ is lineary ordered.
        \item        $ \forall n \in \mathbb{N} \quad (n , <  )  $ is Well ordered.
        \item        $ (\mathbb{N} , <  )  $ is Well ordered.
        \item       $ \Big( \forall x \in \mathbb{N} \quad   \exists y \in \mathbb{N} \quad  x < y \Big)  $ (doesn't have right endpoint)
    \end{enumerate}

    proof.

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

        \have  {2} { 0 \not< 0 }         \by{$def \emptyset $}{}
        \open
        \have  {2} {n\in \mathbb{N} }                                   \by{$acp $}{}
        \open
        \have  {2} { n \not< n }                                   \by{$acp $}{}
        \open
        \have  {2} { s(n) < s(n) }                                 \by{$acp $}{}
        \open
        \have  {2} { s(n) \in n }                                    \by{$acp $}{}
        \have  {2} {  n \in s(n)  }                                 \by{$ \cup I $}{}
        \have  {2} {  n \in n  }                                       \by{$ Transitive(\mathbb{N}) $}{}
        \have  {2} {\bot}                                              \by{$ 3 ,7 $}{}
        \close
        \open
        \have  {2} { s(n) \in \{ n \} }                              \by{$acp $}{}
        \have  {2} { s(n) = n  }
        \have  {2} { s(n) \not< s(n) }                               \by{$= E $}{}
        \have  {2} {\bot}                                              \by{$ 4 ,11 $}{}
        \close
        \have  {2} {\bot}                                             \by{$ \cup E $}{}
        \close
        \have  {2} { s(n) \not< s(n) }                                 \by{$ \neg I $}{}
        \close
        \close
        \have  {2} {\forall n \in \mathbb{N} \quad  n \not< n }         \by{ Induction theorem }{}

    \end{fitchproof}

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

        \have  {2} {\forall n , m ,k  \in \mathbb{N} \quad  n < m < k  \to n < k }         \by{ Induction theorem }{}

    \end{fitchproof}

    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}\textbf{Induction ,second vrsion}
    $ \Big( \forall  k < n \quad  P(k) \Big) \to P(n)  /\therefore \forall n\in \mathbb{N} \quad P(n)$
    \\
    proof.

\end{theorem}

\section{The Recurtion theorem}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Tuple (new definition)-Finite sequence} is a function ``$a : n \to V$'' s.t. $n \in  \mathbb{N}$ we know we can show this
    by ``$(a_i)_{i \in n}$'' we can also show it by ``$(a_0 , a_1 ,a_2 , ... , a_{n-1})$''
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Sequence} is a function ``$a : \mathbb{N} \to V$''  we know we can show this
    by ``$(a_i)_{i \in \mathbb{N}}$'' we can also show it by ``$(a_i)_{i = 0}^{\infty}$''
\end{definition}

now we can redefine ``Cartesian product''. we define finite Cartesian product
``$\prod_{i \in n} A_i := \{ (a_i)_{i \in n} \mid a_i \in A_i \}$'' and define
infinite Cartesian product ``$\prod_{i \in \mathbb{N}} A_i := \{ (a_i)_{i \in
            \mathbb{N}} \mid a_i \in A_i \}$'' and define Cartesian product in general way
``$\prod_{i \in I} A_i := \{ (a_i)_{i \in I} \mid a_i \in A_i \}$''. and you
can easily see that from the definition of ``$B^A$'', ``$A^n$'' is the set of
all $n$-length finite sequences of $a \in A$ and ``$A^\mathbb{N}$'' is the set
of all infinite sequences of $a \in A$.

\begin{theorem}[Recursion]
    For any set $A$, any $a \in A$, and any function $g : A \times \mathbb{N} \to A$,
    there is a unique function $f : \mathbb{N} \to A$ such that:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt}
        \item $f(0) = a$.
        \item $\forall n \in \mathbb{N} \quad f(s(n)) = g(f(n),n)$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}

    Define

    \[
        Compute_n(t_n) :  t_n  : s(n) \to A
        \ \land\ t_n(0) = a \ \land\ \forall k < n \ \big( t(s(k)) = g(t(k),k) \big) .
    \]

    \[
        F := \Big\{ t_n \ \Big|\ Compute_n(t_n) \Big\}.
    \]
    \textbf{Claim 0: $ \forall n \in \mathbb{N} \quad \exists t_{n}\in F$.}

    \textbf{Claim 1: $F \neq \emptyset$.}

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

        \have {12} { t_0 \in F}

        \have {13} {F\neq\emptyset}

    \end{fitchproof}

    Define

    \[
        f := \bigcup F.
    \]

    \textbf{Claim 2: $f$ is a function.}

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

        \open
        \have {1} {t_m,t_n\in F}
        \open
        \have {2} {Dom(t_m)=s(m)}
        \have {3} {Dom(t_n)=s(n)}
        \open
        \have {4} {m \le n}

        \have {5} {t_m(0)=a}
        \have {6} {t_n(0)=a}
        \have {7} {t_m(0)=t_n(0)}

        \open
        \have {12} { k < m }
        \open
        \have {8} {t_m(k)=t_n(k)}
        \have {9} {g(t_m(k),k)=g(t_n(k),k)}
        \have {9} {t_m(s(k))=g(t_m(k),k)}
        \have {10} {t_n(s(k))=g(t_n(k),k)}
        \have {11} {t_m(s(k))=t_n(s(k))}
        \close

        \have {12} {\forall k \leq m \quad t_m (k) = t_n (k)}
        \close
        \close
        \have {13} {t_m \subseteq t_n \lor t_n \subseteq t_m}
        \close

        \close
        \have {14} {\forall t_m , t_n \in F
            \quad
            (t_m\subseteq t_n \lor t_n\subseteq t_m)}

        \have {15} {Compatible(F)}
        \have {20} {Func(f)}
    \end{fitchproof}

    \medskip

    \textbf{Claim 3: $\text{Dom}(f) = \mathbb{N}$ and $\text{Range}(f) \subseteq A$.}

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

        \open
        \have {21} {n\in\mathbb{N}}
        \have {22} {\exists t_n\in F}
        \have {23} {n\in Dom(t_n)}
        \have {25} {n\in Dom(f)}
        \close

        \open
        \have {27} {n\in Dom(f)}
        \open
        \have {28} { t_m \in F }
        \have {28} { n \in Dom(t_m)}
        \have {29} {Dom(t_m)=s(m)}
        \have {30} {n\in s(m)}
        \have {31} {n\in\mathbb{N}}
        \close
        \have {31} {n\in\mathbb{N}}
        \close

        \have {33} {Dom(f)=\mathbb{N}}

        \open
        \have {37} {x\in Range(f)}
        \have {34} {(n,x)\in f}
        \open
        \have {35} { t_m \in F }
        \have {35} { (n,x)\in t}
        \have {36} {t_m : s(m)\to A}
        \have {37} {x\in A}
        \close
        \have {37} {x\in A}
        \close

        \have {38} {Range(f) \subseteq A}

    \end{fitchproof}

    \medskip

    \textbf{Claim 4: $f$ satisfies the recursion equations.}

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

        \have {27} {f(0)=a}
        \open
        \have {21} {n\in\mathbb{N}}
        \have {22} {\exists t_{n+1}\in F}
        \have {23} {t_{n+1}(s(n))
            =
            g(t_{n+1}(n),n)}
        \have {24} {t_{n+1}\subseteq f}
        \have {25} {f(s(n))
            =
            g(f(n),n)}
        \close

        \have {26} {\forall n\in\mathbb{N}
            \quad
            f(s(n))
            =
            g(f(n),n)}

    \end{fitchproof}

    \medskip

    \textbf{Claim 5 : Uniqueness.}

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

        \open
        \have {28} {h:\mathbb{N}\to A}
        \have {29} {h(0)=a}
        \have {30} {\forall n\in\mathbb{N}
            \quad
            h(s(n))
            =
            g(h(n),n)}

        \have {31} {f(0)=h(0)}

        \open
        \have {32} {f(n)=h(n)}
        \have {33} {g(f(n),n) = g(h(n),n) }
        \have {33} {f(s(n)) = g(f(n),n)}
        \have {34} {h(s(n)) = g(h(n),n)}
        \have {35} {f(s(n))=h(s(n))}
        \close

        \have {36} {\forall n\in\mathbb{N}
            \quad
            f(n)=h(n)}

        \have {37} {f=h}
        \close

        \have {38} {\exists !f:\mathbb{N}\to A
            \quad
            \Big(
            f(0)=a
            \land
            \forall n\in\mathbb{N}
            \quad
            f(s(n))
            =
            g(f(n),n)
            \Big)}

    \end{fitchproof}

    \medskip

    Therefore, there exists a unique function $f : \mathbb{N} \to A$ such that
    $f(0) = a$ and $\forall n \in \mathbb{N} \ f(s(n)) = g(f(n),n)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Parametric Recursion Theorem]
    For any sets ``$A,P$'', any function ``$a:P\to A$'', and any function
    ``$g:P\times A\times \mathbb{N}\to A$'', there is a unique function
    ``$f:P\times\mathbb{N}\to A$'' such that
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt}

        \item
              \[
                  \forall p\in P \quad f(p,0)=a(p)
              \]

        \item
              \[
                  \forall p\in P \ \forall n\in\mathbb{N}
                  \quad
                  f\big(p,s(n)\big)=g\big(p,f(p,n),n\big)
              \]
    \end{enumerate}

    \begin{proof}

        Fix $p\in P$.

        Define

        \[
            G_p(x,n):=g(p,x,n).
        \]

        \textbf{Claim 1 :}
        $G_p:A\times\mathbb{N}\to A$.

        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

            \open
            \have {1} {p\in P}
            \open
            \have {2} {(x,n)\in A\times\mathbb{N}}
            \have {3} {g(p,x,n)\in A}
            \have {4} {G(p)(x,n)\in A}
            \close
            \have {5} {G(p):A\times\mathbb{N}\to A}
            \close

            \have {6} {\forall p\in P
                \quad
                G(p):A\times\mathbb{N}\to A}

        \end{fitchproof}

        \medskip

        For every $p\in P$, by the ordinary Recursion Theorem applied to $a(p)\in A$
